<!-- p. 4 -->

Advances in Science, Technology & Innovation (ASTI) is a series of peer-reviewed books based
on important emerging research that redefines the current disciplinary boundaries in science,
technology, and innovation (ASTI) in order to develop integrated concepts for sustainable
development. It not only discusses the progress made towards securing more resources,
allocating smarter solutions, and rebalancing the relationship between nature and people, but
also provides in-depth insights from comprehensive research that addresses the 17 sustainable
development goals (SDGs) as set out by the UN for 2030.
The series promotes the creation and development of viable solutions for a sustainable future
and a positive societal transformation with the help of integrated and innovative science-based
approaches. Including interdisciplinary contributions, it presents innovative approaches and
highlights how they can best support both economic and sustainable development, through
better use of data, more effective institutions, and global, local, and individual action, for the
welfare of all societies.
The series particularly features conceptual and empirical contributions from various
interrelated fields of science, technology, and innovation, with an emphasis on digital
transformation, that focus on providing practical solutions to ensure food, water, and energy
security to achieve the SDGs. It also presents new case studies offering concrete examples of
how to resolve sustainable urbanization and environmental issues in different regions of the
world. The series is intended for professionals in research and teaching, consultancies and
industry, and government and international organizations. Published in collaboration with
IEREK.
The series draws on the best research papers from various IEREK’s international conferences,
while external conference proceedings proposals are no longer accepted as direct publications.
The series does invite significant expansions of selected papers previously submitted to
conferences. The Editors’ role should be evident in the reviews through an intensive doubleblind peer review process, helping authors with edits, and collaborating with them to
demonstrate great academic significance through clear research findings and innovative
solutions that go well beyond a typical conference submission.
The ASTI series welcomes external proposals and requests to publish edited books,
contributed volumes, monographs, and textbooks. With the commitment to the
aforementioned instructions and policy.
The ASTI series has now been accepted for Scopus (September 2020). All content published
in this series will start appearing on the Scopus site in early 2021.
ASTI’s editorial office helps authors and editors in producing work of the highest possible
quality. For any assistance for those interested in submitting proposals please contact the
editorial office at asti.serties@ierek.com

Preface

In an era marked by profound transitions—climatic, social, political, and technological—the
role of heritage has shifted from passive preservation to active engagement with the complex
processes of change. Architectural and urban heritage, once regarded primarily as static sym
bols of the past, are now increasingly understood as dynamic agents in shaping inclusive,
resilient, and sustainable futures. This book arises from that evolving perception.
The Conservation and Sustainable Development of Architectural and Urban Heritage sur
veys the challenges and opportunities of rethinking heritage in modes that are both contextsensitive and forward-looking. The focus on military, modern, and industrial landscapes—each
with their own histories of contested memory, obsolescence, and transformation—poses a fer
tile ground for re-examining how we define value, negotiate authenticity, and imagine adaptive
reuse.
The structure of this book shows this multidimensional inquiry. The first section titled
“Towards New and Inclusive Visions for Military Heritage” reconsiders military heritage
through inclusive interpretive frameworks, adaptive reuse, and integrative planning. It mani
fests how fortifications, prisons, and state-owned assets can be transformed into public assets
while acknowledging their complex histories. Then, the second session introduces arising digi
tal methodologies, emphasizing on the potential of geospatial technologies, 3D modeling, and
integrated data systems in documenting and managing military cultural landscapes.
Another group of contributions focus on industrial heritage, critically addressing the ten
sions between reuse and preservation and examining regeneration strategies that foreground
social sustainability and contextual responsiveness. In some others contributions provide criti
cal “Reflections on the Protection of Modern Heritage”—often overlooked or undervalued—
probing its cultural significance and the sociopolitical hardships of its conservation. The final
panel of studies addresses material and energy efficiency concerns in preserving twentiethcentury architecture, situating conservation within broader environmental and technological
transformations.
This collection clusters contributions from various fields and geographies, displaying a
joint responsibility to reimagining heritage not only as a legacy to be protected but also as a
living resource to be activated. The authors pose critical reflections, advanced tools, and practi
cal case studies that challenge conventional boundaries and encourage cross-sectoral
dialogue.
This volume is expected to serve as a resource for researchers, practitioners, policymakers,
and students engaged in heritage conservation and urban regeneration. More particularly, we
hope it contributes to an expanding discourse that perceives heritage not as a burden of the
past, but as a catalyst for future-oriented design, social cohesion, and environmental
stewardship.

Cagliari, Italy Donatella Rita Fiorino
Portsmouth, UK Tarek Teba
Cagliari, Italy Elisa Pilia

Acknowledgments

The editors of this book would like to express our appreciation to all members of the staff and
the scientific committee for their tremendous efforts and contributions to the development of
this book. We are particularly grateful for the tremendous effort the reviewers made to support
and promote the academic quality of the book. The editors would like to thank all partners
including the University of Portsmouth, University of Cagliari, Polytechnic University of the
Philippines Manila, SIRA, the Italian Society for Architectural Conservation/Restoration,
ICOFORT—the ICOMOS International Scientific Committee on Fortifications and Military
Heritage, the Italian Association for Archaeological and Industrial Heritage, the Italian Institute
of Castles, and the Italian Ministry of Defence (GENIODIFE), for their invaluable support and
involvement, which enabled this interdisciplinary and cross-border academic and professional
output to see the light.
IEREK would like to thank the book editors: Prof. Donatella Rita Fiorino, who is an
Associate Professor in Architectural Restoration, Department of Civil-Environmental and
Architectural Engineering, University of Cagliari, Italy; Dr. Tarek Teba, who is an Associate
Professor in Architectural Heritage, Portsmouth School of Architecture, University of
Portsmouth, UK; and Prof. Elisa Pilia, who is an Assistant Professor in Architectural
Restoration, Department of Civil-Environmental and Architectural Engineering, University of
Cagliari, Italy; and their team for their contribution and providing scientific and logistical sup
port. IEREK takes pride in being an institution that amasses a highly qualified and competent
team who worked tirelessly for months to make this conference what it is today, in hopes of
covering the topic of heritage conservation and maintenance against degradation factors.
Finally, we cannot overlook the prominent role undertaken by our editors and reviewers, who
made it their duty to help this institution in spreading knowledge to the masses.

In this way, one can see that the forts and military facili
ties that constitute the central attributes of the heritage func
tion as a component of a human landscape that utilizes the
2024 natural environment for a specific purpose. One of the unique
human landscapes that utilize the natural environment is a
landscape related to war. The First and Second World Wars
that occurred in the Twentieth Century left a significant mark
on human history. The battlefields and related cemeteries
were considered components that reflect the attributes of a
‘war landscape’. Not only during the First and Second World
Wars of the Twentieth Century, but also in the eighteenth and
nineteenth centuries, and even before that, there were phe
nomena of cities and naval ports being fortified for defensive
purposes. The Koblenz fortification system (Upper Middle
Rhine Valley, Germany, 2002) and the Capital fortifications
of Hanyang (Republic of Korea, 2012) are representative
examples of warscapes (Kim, 2014).
Meanwhile, warscapes can be interpreted as places that
both soldiers and civilians encounter in their daily lives in
the context of armed conflict or the pursuit of a permanent
defence system. Military facilities such as military bases,
shipyards, airbases, communication complexes, barracks,
and training centres occupy large areas in many countries.
They are sometimes interpreted as places of mourning and
1 commemorating specific battles, expeditions, and conflicts.

perimeters (boundaries, city walls) for major facilities that

3.2 Understanding Military Cultural
Landscape through Case Studies

Fortifications and military heritage primarily protect residen
tial areas during peacetime and provide effective defence and
offence during emergencies. These fortifications were con
structed in various forms, including points, lines, and planes,
and they collectively established an efficient defence system
through their interrelated designs, which were influenced by
the prevailing political and social structures. The develop
ment of this defence system was complex, evolving in
response to advancements in technology and construction
methods. This paper examines cases that exemplify the key
functional attributes of military cultural landscapes. To
achieve this, we reanalysed cases listed as World Heritage
sites or included on tentative World Heritage lists, highlight
ing their significance as military cultural landscapes.
On the other hand, the Guidance on the preparation of
retrospective Statements of Outstanding Universal Value
(OUV) (UNESCO World Heritage Centre, 2024) explains

<!-- p. 27 -->

16 D. W. Cho

military landscape. This site harmoniously integrates the
water environment for gardens and agricultural land man
agement with the walled city on the adjacent hills while also
serving as a significant component of the region’s defensive
capabilities.
The Diyarbakır Fortress and Hevsel Gardens Cultural
Landscape (Türkiye, 2015) is situated on an escarpment in
the Upper Tigris River Basin. This fortified city and its asso
ciated landscape have served as a significant centre and
regional capital throughout the Hellenistic, Roman, Sassanid,
and Byzantine periods, continuing through the Islamic and
Ottoman eras to the present day. The site features the impres
sive Diyarbakır City Walls, which extend 5800 m and include
numerous towers, gates, buttresses, and 63 inscriptions from
various historical periods. The fertile Hevsel Gardens also
connect the city to the Tigris River, providing essential food
and water resources. The City Walls and evidence of their
damage, repair, and reinforcement since the Roman period
offer a compelling physical and visual testament to the
region’s rich history. Key attributes of this property include
the İçkale (Inner Castle), the Diyarbakır City Walls (also
known as the Dişkale or Outer Castle), along with its towers,
gates, and inscriptions, the Hevsel Gardens, the Tigris River
and Valley, and the Ten-Eyed Bridge. The ability to view the
walls within their urban and landscape contexts is signifi
cant, as are the hydrological and natural resources that
enhance the functional and visual qualities of the property
(UNESCO World Heritage Centre, World Heritage List,
1488).

Interpretation of the Military Cultural Landscapes of the ICOMOS Guidelines on Fortifications and Military Heritage (2021) 17

In particular, the cultural landscape of the Upper Middle 4 6
Rhine Valley, which has facilitated cultural exchanges 2024
between the Mediterranean region and Northern Europe, is
shaped by a combination of topographical and geological
factors, as well as human-made elements such as settlements,
transportation infrastructure, and land use that have devel
oped over the past 2000 years. This area represents an organ
ically evolved cultural landscape. Specifically, the property
encompasses more than 60 small towns and settlements, 40
castles and fortresses, terraced vineyards, and diverse geo
logical formations (UNESCO World Heritage Centre, World
Heritage list, 1066).

3.2.3 Capital Fortifications of Hanyang
(Republic of Korea, Continuing Evolved
Landscape)
Experiences gained through wars became valuable founda
tions for the development of national defence and the estab
lishment of fortresses with different characteristics.

3
5

5

The ‘Capital Fortifications of Hanyang’ is located in the
centre of the capital city of Seoul and the border of Gyeonggi
Province of the Republic of Korea. The geographical scope
of the property is the urban area and mountainous area
between the Jungnangcheon Stream and Bulgwangcheon
Stream in the north of the Han River, the south of the Imjin
River, and the east-west direction. The ‘Capital Fortifications
of Hanyang’ are located from south to north with the
Hanyangdoseong Capital City Wall, Tangchundaeseong
Defence Wall, and Bukhansanseong Mountain Fortress. The
‘Capital Fortifications of Hanyang’ was built along a ridge of

<!-- p. 29 -->

2018

mountainous terrain. All three walls of the ‘Capital
Fortifications of Hanyang’ are typical because they were
built along a mountainous ridge surrounding the flat land
(UNESCO World Heritage Centre, World Heritage Tentative
list, 6652).

3.2.4 Rajgad Fort of Maratha Military
Architecture in Maharashtra (India,
Associative Landscape)
Integrating the landscape, terrain, and physiographic charac
teristics, particularly distinctive to the Sahyadri mountain
ranges, the Konkan coast, the Deccan Plateau, and the
Eastern Ghats in the Indian peninsula, has resulted in a num
ber of fortified landscapes varying in hierarchies, scales, and
typological features(UNESCO World Heritage Centre,
World Heritage Tentative List, 6533).
In particular, Rajgad Fort was the first capital of the
Maratha Kingdom.
‘Forts are the very basis for the existence of a kingdom.
Forts themselves are the kingdom, forts are the origin of the
kingdom, forts are the real treasure of the kingdom, forts are
the basis of an army, forts are the wealth of the kingdom,
forts are our saviour and key of defence’ (Shikha & Rima,
2021).
It meant the philosophy of how the Maratha kingdom
dealt with the forts and also reflected their identity in the
kingdom. Rajgad Fort is based on the Mountain Murumbadev
(the Mountain of Murumba Goddess). To the Marathas, the
2024 goddess Murumba was a symbol of protection. Their faith
did not stop at mere belief; they found meaning in the natural
environment and eventually built a strong and unconquerable
the city and the city’s defence system. Bukhansanseong fortress system that was enough to make it the kingdom’s
Fortress, which was created by connecting the numerous capital. It can be interpreted as an associated and symbolic
peaks of Bukhansan Mountain, is a masterpiece created by landscape where the trust relationship between God, the
nature and humans. Numerous literary works have continued King, and the people was solid. The following citation gives
to express this symbolic landscape and participate in activi testimony to strengthen the symbolic military landscapes of
ties to protect local heritage communities. The components Rajgad. Contemporary Mughal source Maasssir-Alamgiri
of the capital fortifications of Hanyang are primarily classi described Rajgad as a ‘Fort of the towered sky, fort spreads
fied as the main attributes for forming the capital defence over 12 kos of land. No one can estimate its height. Its thick
system as follows: In Bukhansanseong Mountain Fortress, jungles and spectre-haunted chasm can traversed only by
the natural environment is more important than the human wind. Only rain can descend from it, no man or animal can’
environment, such as military facilities, administrative facili (Shikha & Rima, 2021).
ties, and religious facilities. In Tangchundaeseong Defense
Wall, the natural environment, such as mountainous terrain,
is much more important than the human environment, which
is composed of military and administrative facilities for pro 7
vision and miliatry material storage. In the Hanyangdoseong
Capital City Wall, the human environment comprises signifi
cant ruling and military attributes such as military and
administrative facilities, which are more important than the
natural environment (Fig. 6).
In this regard, the capital fortifications of Hanyang reflect
the development stages of the capital defence system of the
Eighteenth Century. These fortresses bear witness to the mil
itary cultural landscape as a capital defence system resulting
from war experience in Korea, and they are well-preserved
until now. Their core functionality remains. They have their
historical value, representing their integrity and Authenticity.
Each of them is designated as a national historic site and well
maintained by each local government with the cooperation
of the Korea Heritage Service.

These investigations have made it possible to define the vari
ous types of mortars, consisting of various types of lime and

In summary, the main restoration works were:

• the removal of various improper structures supported or
located on top of the curtain wall;
• the elimination of weed vegetation on the wall with man
ual, mechanical and Endo therapy systems, removal of
residues and earths;
• low pressure washing of wall surfaces;
• the consolidation of the wall faces with scuci e cuci oper
ations and restoration of wall joints;
• integration and reconstruction of masonry structures with
recovered materials or on-site (stones and bricks).

These are minimally invasive works, carried out using tra
ditional techniques Di Sivo et al. 2021). The following works

systems;
• the execution of a lime underlay on top with restoration

The research delves into this unique former military site
with a history of repeated abandonment. By tracing its main
events from construction to the present, the study aims to n.d.
investigate the challenges and opportunities in valorising
public heritage to foster its active protection. The case is
notable as it exemplifies the complex dynamics of historic
building recovery and its implications, a topic of great rele
vance and urgent need for immediate action.
The analysis employs a critical and multidisciplinary
approach, utilising various qualitative and quantitative meth
ods, including scientific and grey literature review, report
analysis, data and project evaluation, archival research, inter 3
view, mapping, site inspection, and photographic survey.
The initial hypothesis posits that if a building can be 2014

<!-- p. 49 -->

Heritage Is Plural. Recovery and Adaptive Reuse of the Polveriera Castelfidardo in Ancona 39

and fortifications. Located centrally on the coast, it develops
from a ridge descending from the Apennines to the Adriatic,
between the Esino and Musone rivers, via hilly terraces that
drop steeply towards the sea. Its potential has emerged since
the foundation of Ankón (from Ἀγκών, “elbow”) in 387 BC
by Doric exiles from Syracuse, who built a mighty wall from
Colle Guasco to the port to protect the settlement from the
Gauls and Piceni (Luchetti, 1996, p. 5; Luchetti & Belogi,
1993, p. 540). The name recalls the elbow-shaped coast, in
whose bend the city is, as noted by Pliny, who attributed its
foundation to the Siculi, as Numana (Plinio il Vecchio, 1489,
III, XI). This population is older and lived in Italy before
Greek was widespread, so the established historiography on
the origin and name of the city has recently been questioned
(Morini, 2020).
Ancona held strategic importance in Roman times. Livy
called it the “Hinge of Italy”. The city walls were extended,
and in 103 AD, Emperor Trajan built a sizeable fortified port.
The Roman Senate honoured him with a triumphal arch
(Luchetti, 1996, p. 5), “because he restored to sailors the saf
est entrance to Italy, having also expanded this port with his
own money”, as the Latin inscription reads. Fortifications
intensified in the following centuries, especially after the
Ottoman conquest of Otranto (1480 AD) and the Sack of
Rome (1527 AD). Under papal rule from 1532, Ancona
transformed into a “fortress city” aligned with modern defen
sive criteria. Military structures were strengthened, and new
ones were built, such as the Cittadella in 1532 and the
Lazzaretto in 1733, equipped with a eight-gun-emplacement
external bulwark (Luchetti, 1996, pp. 5–13).

tity for Ancona was estimated to be 500,000 kg, but the

The Polveriera Castelfidardo could stock up to 200,000 kg
of explosives and was an “alla prova” powder magazine (i.e.,
bomb-proof): that is, it could withstand the test of a bombing
(Bruschi, 2011, p. 72). It has a rectangular plan measuring
26 × 13 m and consists of three adjacent longitudinal rooms,
barrel-vaulted and covered by a 45° pitched roof covered in
bricks. To mitigate the danger of explosions, it was built on
the slopes of Monte Cardeto in a hollow artificially depressed
through excavation and the construction of earth dams sup
ported by stone walls clad in bichrome bricks with decora
1993 tive motifs. It was externally surrounded by 4.40 m thick
perimeter structures called “blinde”—then demolished—to
bury it during sieges. Internally, a 2 m layer of sandy soil was

structure, extinguishing the flames (Bruschi, 2011, pp. 72–73;
Morando played a pivotal role in modernising and enhancing Giovannini, 2014, p. 78).
the defensive system and faced a crucial aspect of the opera The load-bearing structure is made of limestone with

Heritage Is Plural. Recovery and Adaptive Reuse of the Polveriera Castelfidardo in Ancona 41

are created of Istrian stone and moulded brick on-site.
Explosives were stored in the largest central room, a single
8 m high space with a mezzanine to increase the storage area,
extending the entire length and 7 m width. On either side
stand two 3 m high rooms, which initially served as connec 10
tors and air circulation galleries to control humidity and pre
vent damage to the gunpowder. For this reason, the main
room was initially equipped with a wooden floor and clad
ding, and ventilation was facilitated by two orders of vents in
the side walls, communicating with the circular air intakes
on the façade and the side galleries, to be closed on humid 2011
days. Rising dampness was prevented by the floor placed on
a barrel-vaulted cavity. The disposal and reuse of rainwater
were managed through drainage wells located at the base of 2024d
the earth dams, which fed the water wells situated in the
extremities of the site (Bruschi, 2011, pp. 72–73; Giovannini,
2014, p. 78).

The 10-year works lasted from 2005 to 2015: they
involved the building and the surrounding area (2005–2008);
much later (2014–2015), the urban infrastructures were built
(Comune di Ancona, 2015). The methods prioritised arti
sanal and sustainable solutions: handcrafting to reproduce
missing elements in Istrian stone; reusing original construc
tion materials recovered on-site; using lime and cocciopesto
mortars, free of cement, and non-industrialised products for
restorations and reintegrations, such as brick flooring
(Giovannini, 2014, pp. 80–82). The main results were:

– the restoration of damaged and structurally compromised
walls without replacing every deteriorated element;

The Polveriera Castelfidardo was not an ordinary explosives
depot but a notable example of architectural innovation. Its
finishes and technological solutions set it apart from tradi
18 tional military structures, making it unique compared with
other nineteenth-century powder magazines built in Italy
(Bruschi, 2011). As a symbol of military modernisation and
regional autonomy, it reflected the political and social
2023 changes, imbued with a significance (from its name) that
2024a 2024b overcame the limits of architecture, unleashing its potential
to connect disciplines and embody the spirit of the time.

The research of the three workshops has been structured
in a unified framework, achieving results from the issues 2003 2021
dealt with on different scales: territorial, urban, and architec
tural. This co-design approach is not configured as a substi 1981 1999
tute for institutional territorial choices but rather as a direct

initiatives for the valorisation of state property compendiums
that are congruent with the needs of the communities that
host them and with the transcalar strategies of regional
planning.

on the disused state-owned cultural heritage, in particular the

3 The Case Study of Ederle Barrack

The current military use enables its accessibility only for
its employees. It is occasionally open for cultural events and
is barely connected with the city centre, especially during
autumn and winter.
Currently, the Barracks have a trapezoidal-shaped
enclosed boundary with a wall about 633 m long, placed to
encircle 16 buildings used for military purposes. Each build
ing included in this area has been studied according to the
above described and consolidated protocol of knowledge
with detailed indirect and direct analyses such as architec
3 tural surveys, study of the material state of conservation,
analysis of the constructive elements through Raumbuch
cataloguing, definition of elements of interest and assess
ment of values and, definition of levels of transformability.

F. Naldi (*) states took place (cf. Foucault, 1975). Certain types of publicSapienza University of Rome, Rome, Italy
e-mail: francesca.naldi@uniroma1.it buildings were, in fact, redesigned and rethought in line with

<!-- p. 70 -->

60 F. Naldi

the philanthropic ideals of the Enlightenment (cf. Beccaria,
1764), which advocated for the recovery of the individual
through rational principles. During these years, the metropo
lis was conceived as a system in which various mechanisms
with specific functional characteristics. The aim was to cre
ate a network of highly specialized hubs within the Bourbon
Kingdom, interconnected by rapid communication routes. 2
Detention structures were adapted to these premises, rede
signed during this period to form ‘useful’ individuals, con
ceived as a fundamental part of the productive process. Thus,
the approach shifted from a punitive one to a reformative
one, aimed at physically and morally rehabilitating prisoners
in order to maximize their productive effectiveness (Buccaro,
1992; cf. Evans, 1970).
The architectural ‘machine’ that best suited these theo
retical principles was the panopticon (Bentham 2009), for
mulated by Jeremy Bentham in 1787, based on a radial
model that allowed for the removal of the physical limita
tions of detention structures and optimized the surveillance
system by incorporating a central observation point for mon
itoring and controlling the prisoners. Behind the democratic
principles that seem to underpin the ideal Benthamian model,
there lie mechanisms of imprisonment and deprivation,
which led R. Dubbini, in 1985, to define the Panopticon as a
«cage that becomes an observatory, theater, machine, the
architecture of a patient and attentive power, which will be 3
more brutal but must be exercised scientifically through sub
tle pedagogy» (Dubbini, 1985. p.34). The prison’s cage is
based on a “dissimmetry of power” between the guard and
the prisoner, imposing on the latter a moral duty to comply
with the rules and participate in productive activities.
From the French decade (1806–1815), during the reign of
Joseph Bonaparte—Napoleon’s brother—and Joachim
2 Architecture and Typological Features Murat, a policy of reusing existing buildings for the establish
of the Bourbon Prisons ment of new public works spread, due to significant expenses
and the number of infrastructures needed for the city.
Specifically, following the suppression of monastic orders
with the Napoleonic decree of 1810, many of the conventual
buildings were repurposed, and some of them being con
verted into prisons. This process of conversion led to the
establishment of prisons whose design was rigidly connected
1 to the architectural characteristics of the pre-existing struc
tures, moving away from new theories on the geometric and
distributive layout of detention facilities. They were primarily
configured with a rigid rectangular layout with a traditional
fortified appearance. An emblematic case of this reuse pro
cess is the Bourbon prison of Santa Maria Capua Vetere. The
detention facility resulted from the suppression of the San
Francesco di Paola complex on September 1809, which had
been severely damaged by the earthquake of July 1805.
During these years, the outbreak of an epidemic and the dis
mantling of the old prisons, combined with the establishment
of a new court in the city, made it necessary to set up a new
prison in the former monastic structure, in accordance with

<!-- p. 71 -->

Bourbon Prison Islands and the Case Study of D’avalos Palace in Procida Knowledge and Restoration of a Challenging Heritage… 61

the engineer of Roads and Bridges Aureliano Maria De Fusco,
who proposed the installation of the new prison in the sup
pressed convent of San Francesco di Paola and the adjacent
cryptoporticus. The quadrangular layout of the complex fol
lowed the scheme of the pre-existing structure and the under
ground rooms, with only one wing being newly constructed.
The construction of the prison proved to be extremely com
plex and troubled, and the long duration of the work made the
project for the provincial prison of Santa Maria outdated and
inadequate, especially in comparison with the plans for the
prisons of Avellino and Campobasso, which, at the urging of
the engineers of the Corps of Roads and Bridges, followed the
new construction norms and panoptic schemes.
Other examples of Bourbon detention architecture sug
gest that the choice of a quadrangular courtyard plan was not
solely linked to the reuse of pre-existing conventual struc
tures but depended on the ability of this scheme to optimize
the functioning of the prison and the control system. Among
these cases is the Bourbon prison in Syracuse, built in 1853,
following the failure of the 1848 insurrectionary uprisings,
which resulted in a significant increase in the number of
arrests (Santuccio, 1997). The new prison, which remained
the royal decree of February 6, 1807, which stipulated that in active until 1991, featured a rectangular plan with an internal
“every jurisdictional capital” (art. 1) and in “every residence square courtyard that allowed for simultaneous visibility of
of the court of appeals” (art. 5), a new detention facility would the entire complex.
be established (Amirante et al., 2018, p. 152). The first project
for the Santa Maria prison dates back to 1816, designed by

<!-- p. 72 -->

62 F. Naldi

schemes: a panoptic scheme (either horseshoe-shaped or
with separate bodies) and a courtyard scheme, derived from
the reuse of conventual complexes, but also recurring in
some newly constructed buildings (Fig. 4). The preference
for the radial model, which best suited the philosophical and
legal theories of the nineteenth century, is evident. However,
the execution of such a complex scheme was not always rep
licable due to lack of space and economic resources. This led
to the choice of the courtyard typology, which was able to
contain construction costs while optimizing the control and
surveillance system.

3 Bourbon ‘Isolated’ Detention
Architectures

From the beginning of the nineteenth century until the uni
fication of Italy, prison policy became particularly profit
able. Within the national territory, island contexts became
privileged places for imprisonment, both for reasons of
security and for the territorial containment of an ‘inconve
nient’ function. It is therefore possible to recognize the
existence of a real «archipelago of detention» (Cocco &
Giannattasio, 2019) in the Mediterranean, capable of opti
mizing the attempt at social separation and deprivation of
freedom. In the context of a typological analysis, island
prisons tend to elude a clear definition of an architectural
model. In most cases, they were the result of the reuse and
extension of pre-existing fortified architecture, which rarely
met the formal criteria promoted by the penal policy of the
time. At the time, many island territories were dotted with

<!-- p. 73 -->

Bourbon Prison Islands and the Case Study of D’avalos Palace in Procida Knowledge and Restoration of a Challenging Heritage… 63

hermitic, introverted and inaccessible structures such as
forts, towers, and bastions. The vocation of these architec
tures as marginal and ‘safe’ places accentuated a process of
resignification of these facilities during the nineteenth cen
tury. From residential fortresses capable of optimizing
defense from the outside to the inside, many of these archi
tectures were transformed into places of detention, protect 5
ing the outside from the inside, where the ‘social enemy’
was imprisoned. The choice to adapt the island’s fortified
architecture also responds to a logic of economic and logis
tical optimisation, since the supply of building materials is
greatly complicated by the territories’ condition of geo
graphical isolation.
Several architectural episodes in the national island
landscape bear witness to this readaptation of prison build
ings. These include the island of Elba, with Fort Longone,
built at the beginning of the seventeenth century under the In June 1809, Anglo-Bourbon troops landed on Ischia,
Spanish viceroyalty of Naples and converted into a prison initiating intense bombardments that caused significant

of the Two Sicilies from the beginning of the eighteenth later, in 1851, became a penal facility for the most prominent
century, when the Neapolitan branch of the Bourbons political opponents of the Bourbons (cf. D’Auria, 2024). As

control of southern Italy. For this reason, during the abandoned and inaccessible today is the original Keep,
Bourbon regime in the mid-nineteenth century, it was owned by a Naples-based company.
decided to extend the policy of imprisonment to the smaller
islands, also due to the overcrowding of prisons on the pen
insula. Among the sites identified by the Crown, the only
one built ex novo was the aforementioned island of
Ventotene, which in 1979 became the site of the Italian
institution that most closely resembled Bentham’s
Panopticon. More widespread, however, was the tendency
to convert existing island fortresses that had been used as
places of confinement during these years.

4 The Phlegraean Islands in the Gulf
of Naples: Three Examples Compared

This process is particularly evident in the Phlegraean Islands
of Ischia, Nisida, and Procida in the Gulf of Naples, which
share a similar historical-evolutionary course. These territo
ries were selected for the construction of castles and for 6
tresses during the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, later
transformed into detention sites, and simultaneously
expanded, under Bourbon rule, before meeting different
fates in the twentieth century.

<!-- p. 74 -->

64 F. Naldi

«the structure of the castle, with its circular layout, was
well suited to the panoptic scheme, allowing direct control
of the rooms arranged within the ring of the building
through the corridor windows facing the center of the court
[…]» (Buccaro, 2017. p.191).
After Italian unification, the complex expanded across the
entire islet, with the construction of new buildings—still
present today—to meet the prison’s new needs. Further
expansion followed in the 1930s, with the construction of
rationalist-style buildings along the island ridge to house a
juvenile reform center. During this time, the sixteenth-
century fortress fell into disuse and began to deteriorate rap
idly, partially dismantled to make way for a reinforced
concrete building in the post-war period. Unlike Ischia,
Nisida remains a detention site with the current Juvenile
Penitentiary Institute; however, the original Piccolomini
Castle (later a penal colony) remains in a state of decay and
abandonment.

5 Procida Island and the Case Study
of D’avalos Palace and the Former
Bourbon Prison

The d’Avalos Palace and former Bourbon prison on the
island of Procida is also in a state of near-total abandonment,
sharing a historical and developmental trajectory similar to
that of the other Phlegraean Islands in the Gulf of Naples.
Like surrounding islands, Procida’s history appears to be
“written in its sea” (cf. Di Liello & Rossi, 2017). Its proxim

<!-- p. 75 -->

Bourbon Prison Islands and the Case Study of D’avalos Palace in Procida Knowledge and Restoration of a Challenging Heritage… 65

ity to the sea made it necessary, in the sixteenth century, for
Cardinal Innico d’Avalos d’Aragona to build his residence-
fortress to defend against Saracen attacks. The cardinal chose
a tuff promontory as the ideal site for the new construction,
located at the island’s highest point near the original medi
eval settlement, previously known as Terra Casata. However,
with the addition of a sixteenth-century bastion to the Palace,
it was renamed Terra Murata. The cardinal’s palace reflects
its dual residential and defensive functions through two 8b
facades characterized by markedly different architectural
styles: one, facing the sea, is mute and severe (with only a
few narrow slits), while the internal facade reflects the
Renaissance residential style, featuring decorative elements
in piperno stone.
The property came into Bourbon possession in the first
half of the eighteenth century, becoming one of the first royal
sites designated by Charles III in 1734, with contributions
from two famous court architects, Ferdinando Fuga and
Carlo Vanvitelli (cf. D’Alessandro et al., 2004). Following
the site’s prolific activity through the eighteenth century,
major political and functional changes occurred, leading to a
radical transformation of the values previously embodied by Decommissioned in 1988 and acquired by the municipal
the Bourbon site. At the beginning of the nineteenth century, ity of Procida in 2013, only part of the complex is occasion
the Palace was used as a military school, and in March 1830, ally open to the public, and due to the limited economic
His Royal Highness Francesco, Vicar of the Kingdom, sustainability of proposed projects, no restoration and
ordered the school spaces to be used for detention, as enhancement plan for the area has yet been approved.
requested by the Ministry of the Interior. This initiative arose
from the critical conditions of Neapolitan prisons, which,
due to spatial limitations, “risked imploding” (Assante, 5.1 The Current State: Conservation Issues
2015). Ferdinand II’s original plan was to create a “Grand of the Insular Palimpsest
Prison” with a panopticon layout, consistent with contempo
rary prison architecture, to simulate an invisible authority 5.1.1 The Historical Stratification
with a central tower and cells arranged radially. There were and the Coexistence of Different

68 F. Naldi

some cases, this degradation results in structural instability.
Finally, an uncontrolled growth of vegetation within the area
further exacerbates the problem, violently encroaching upon

5.1.3 Damage Mechanisms of Coastal 3
Structures

Despite the lack of economic sustainability for project
funding, studies and knowledge about the Procidan complex
have continued to develop in recent years, in an attempt to
raise awareness about the protection of a place that, although
it evokes painful memories, is emblematic of a highly sig
nificant historical and evolutionary path in southern Italy.
One of the latest initiatives in this direction is the training
workshop promoted by the School of Specialisation in
Architectural Heritage and Landscape (SSBAP) of the
Federico II University of Naples, held in October 2022 under
the direction of Professor Renata Picone. The experience
was divided into a series of activities of direct knowledge of
the artifact through geometric and diagnostic survey, and a
subsequent reflection on the possible paths of resignification
of the complex.
The systematization of the documentation that character
izes the study of the Procidan artifact allows us today to
reflect more clearly and consciously on the various aspects
of the physical and functional conservation of the artifact.
1 The conservation choices must be evaluated in parallel with
the functional and perceptive choices. Restoration interven
tions on the different building components must align with a
broader vision for enhancing and enabling access to the com
plex, with the ultimate goal being an action that cannot be
fragmented but should follow a coordinated approach in both
restoration and management phases. The overall objective is
to safeguard the narrative strength of the complex through
architectural and functional decisions.
5.2 The Palimpsest Returns
to the Community: Functional
Directions for the Procidan Complex

The effectiveness of the strategic action primarily con
cerns the possibility of connecting the complex with the sur
rounding territory, both physically and functionally. The
former prison complex is currently separated from the adja
cent urban fabric, making it a true ‘island within an island’.
Therefore, the design aims to identify new pathways that can
connect the island’s main attractions to the Procida complex.
At present, the only access road to the site is Via Salita
Castello, which starts from the Marina Grande Port and leads
to the historic village of Terra Murata. Another proposal for
connection involves restoring the historic Via Principessa

1 4 Categorisation of the Military
Heritage in the UK Listed on the MoD
Disposal List

The purpose of categorisation is (1) to identify different
types of defence heritage sites in the MoD disposal list, (2) to
assess how the characteristics or values of military structures
can affect their reuse and regeneration, and (3) to evaluate
their conservation status. The categorisation and analysis of
the UK MoD disposal list aim to shed light on the most chal
lenging military heritage typologies that are not adequately
addressed in terms of preservation and conservation as well
as their regeneration potential. This investigation classified
military sites and examined how the attributes of military
structures influence their potential for reuse and regeneration.

<!-- p. 88 -->

78 S. Pashaei and T. Teba

As one of the UK’s largest landowners, the MoD’s estate
comprises over 4000 sites (UK Parliament, 2016), encom
passing a broad spectrum of military buildings. The complex
nature and diverse range of military heritage underscore the
importance of understanding its typologies. Therefore, this
section focuses on the MoD disposal list published in March
2023 and explores military structures across various catego
ries, aiming to identify distinctive characteristics and types
of sites listed for disposal by the MoD in the UK. In order to
determine the site’s current location and accessibility,
Historic England, the local council archive and existing maps 4.1 Categorisation of the Military
were consulted. Installations

The Military Installation refers to the overarching category
2 that includes a site/ sites and associated buildings used by the
military for various purposes, usually defensive. The sub-
categories include: Barracks, which are buildings used to
house military personnel along with supporting facilities like
refectories, mess rooms, hospitals, schools, and gymnasia.
Defence Research Establishments are complex buildings

1
2

3

<!-- p. 89 -->

Military Heritage Conservation and Regeneration: A Categorisation Approach to Identify the Most Challenging Military Heritage… 79

where military technologies and weaponry are developed
and tested. Fuel Depots refer to buildings or sites used for
the storage and distribution of fuel for military purposes. A
Military Airfield is the landing and take-off area for military
aircraft, which often includes ancillary structures and build
ings for the maintenance and storage of aircraft, defence of Barracks
the site, accommodation of staff, and control of airspace, 4
among other purposes. A Military Base is a building or
group of buildings, often surrounded by a system of fortifica
tions, used as a residential and training site by members of an
armed force. A Naval Dockyard refers to a naval base where
naval ships are built, repaired, docked or converted warships
and are manned by civilian engineers and workers and
administered by engineer duty officers. These dockyards are
crucial for the operational readiness of the Navy, providing
essential services such as dry docking, maintenance, and
logistical support for naval vessels. Ordnance Depots are
buildings and/or areas used by the armed forces for the stor
age and issuing of military stores and materials. A Prisoner
of War Camp is a facility designed for the containment of
service personnel captured in war. Radar Station refers to a
building or site incorporating radar equipment used for
detecting the presence of enemy aircraft or ships (Historic
England, 2021).

plexity, 20% are complex sites with multiple buildings, while
Fortifications are defensive structures, often permanent,
designed to protect against enemy attacks. Subtypes include
Artillery Forts, fortified sites with purpose-built emplace
ments for artillery; Castles, fortified noble residences from
the mediaeval or early post-mediaeval periods, characterised
by curtain walls and gatehouses; Hillforts, hilltop enclosures
with substantial ramparts and ditches, now often part of rec
reational or agricultural landscapes; Roman Forts, stan
dardised military bases from the Roman period, comprising
barracks, victualling, and command structures; and Town
Walls, fortified barriers that historically encircled towns or
cities for defence. These subtypes illustrate the evolution of
defensive architecture and strategy over time (Historic
2018 England, 2021).

<!-- p. 95 -->

Military Heritage Conservation and Regeneration: A Categorisation Approach to Identify the Most Challenging Military Heritage… 85

12 tural features such as thick walls and underground structures,
resist easy conversion to modern uses, creating a tension
between preservation and functionality (Historic England,
2017). Many fortifications are in strategically selected loca
tions, making them less accessible and thus less commer
cially attractive, with costly infrastructure needed to enhance
accessibility. Additionally, environmental factors accelerate
The redevelopment of historic fortifications in the UK structural degradation, requiring expensive and specialised

and adaptation. These sites, often built with unique architec constraints further complicate redevelopment, as these often-

listed buildings often demand adherence to stringent conser

The challenges faced in redeveloping fortifications, as
seen with Fort Blockhouse, align closely with key categori

2024a servation demands. The fort’s urban-coastal location offers
b potential for public engagement but also poses environmen

Additionally, its historical significance and Grade II-listed
2022 structures require strict conservation measures, highlighting
the financial and logistical burdens associated with such
projects. The complexity of the site, with its defensive design
and multiple buildings, further underscores the need for sub
stantial infrastructure upgrades to make it accessible for
civilian or mixed-use purposes, aligning with the broader
challenges noted in the categorisation analysis.

knowledge of the geographic relationships that landscapes,
as cultural and environmental heritage, establish with the

this framework, the global topic of the conservation of cul

document and analyze the environmental and anthropic
relationships that characterize Cultural Heritage (CH)
(Stubbs, 2009). Here, knowledge enrichment is a necessary
prerequisite for continuous heritage conservation strategies
(Della Torre, 2003), where the regular input of data requires
the researchers to focus on developing consolidated and
accurate methodologies for the automation of analysis, val
idation, and semantic classification of the collected datas 1903
ets. The contribution of geomatics science, long applied to
the documentation of heritage, provides significant
advancements in the management of conservation pro
cesses and in supporting cultural dissemination. This is
achieved through the acquisition, processing, and visual
ization of data, which can then be further developed into
semantic/parametric three-dimensional modeling processes
(Brumana et al., 2019). Specifically, recent research themes
in geomatics and computer vision applied to Cultural 2003
Heritage have focused not only on improving technologies 2008
and methodologies for data acquisition but also on consoli
dating, structuring, and organizing the acquired data
(Colucci et al., 2021). The semantic classification of
unstructured data is a crucial issue for geomatic disciplines.
Here, the technological advancements of sensors in recent
decades have allowed the enrichment and expeditiousness
of acquired spatial data, leading the geomatics community
to question the impact of data processing. This appears par
ticularly crucial, where the analysis, the semantic and rela

according to the specific application context. The landscape
and built heritage documentation and mapping framework 2008

and semantic content enrichment being especially crucial
issues in such a complex and fragile context. In this frame
work, airborne Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) and
spaceborne imagery techniques are particularly suitable for
the mapping of extended sites.

Military Digital Landscapes. An Overview of GeoAI Methodologies for Built and Landscape Heritage Preliminary Documentation 95

structed along territorial borders and major lines of commu
nication. Many of these were promptly abandoned, others
adapted to new military needs, and others were newly built.
Starting from the sixteenth century, new elements enriched
the complex medieval defensive system, such as fortified
walls and coastal towers erected to protect from Saracen
invasions (Vacca, 2015; Murru, 2015). During the eighteenth
century, with the Kingdom of Sardinia-Piedmont, building
new royal strongholds, forts, and bastions was carried out
with reinforcement along the coast. During the nineteenth
century, building work focused on the construction of mili
tary barracks to maintain and restore public order in the
newly founded Kingdom of Italy. The last step of construc
tion is represented by the extensive military production dur

ing the World War II: batteries, bunkers, strongholds,
pillboxes, air-raid shelters, and watchtowers (Mameli &
Sanjust, 2015; Martinez-Medina & Sanjust, 2015).

2.2 Data Structuring: Traditional Machine 2.2.2 Deep Learning Models

2.2.1 Machine Learning Classifiers
At the moment of acquiring big spatial data, it should be
underlined that this data comes in an unstructured form, and
the urgent need to automate the processing of this data in the
most efficient way is crucial. When dealing with machine
learning, discussing supervised and unsupervised approaches
is usual. In the supervised approach, we use reference ground
truth data generated by human knowledge. These are given
to a machine learning algorithm or a neural network as train 2021

scape contexts. In this framework, the present research has
extensively clarified that the numerosity of heritage artifacts
or features and reference datasets is usually not compatible
with DL methodologies. In this sense, the study identifies
these challenges and highlights synthetic data generation as
one of the possible solutions. In fact, synthetic data could
complement real data or even replace it, allowing generaliza
tion models that might be trained to complete and succeed in
several time-consuming tasks.
Moreover, a key advantage of this study was the leverag
ing of GUI GIS software, which has simplified the manage
ment of both spatial data and predictive models within a
single software environment, enabling non-expert users to
access such tools. Specifically, user-friendly GIS, coding,
and library management platforms (e.g., ArcGIS Pro,
MATLAB, Anaconda) embed GeoAI toolboxes, enabling
both traditional machine learning and deep neural networks.
These platforms support a complete workflow within a sin
gle software environment, also by leveraging code from open
digital repositories such as GitHub. Nonetheless, theoretical
and methodological knowledge is again crucial for the devel
opment of meaningful applications. In fact, such systems do
not fully exploit the most current and contemporary research
advancements for remote sensing data, thus requiring deeper
technical expertise.

3.3 3D Metric Survey and Modelling
Results

The 3D metric survey of the Gardetta plateau aimed to docu
ment both the three Opere inner and outdoor areas and the
entire valley in which they are located. Moving from these
targets, the survey campaign was planned to integrate differ 2023
ent methods, beginning with establishing an accurate topo

national standard UTM WGS 84 (32 N) is the adopted coor
dinate reference system.

architectural and landscape scales. Specifically, UAV

up the possibility of reconstructing their missing parts.
Specifically, since these structures had military and defen
sive purposes but no weapon and instrument has ever been
installed, the research aimed to reconstruct, for each defen
sive position, which type of weapon and other apparatus
should have been installed (Fig. 4). In comparison to the
other fortifications in the European military landscape of the
same period, the Vallo Alpino is a particular case. In fact, in
contrast to the situation in the rest of Europe, the Vallo Alpino
Opere had to be designed to accommodate the already exist
ing armament and commonly used by Regio Esercito so, to
achieve this goal, casemates, turrets, and armour plates were
built, together with the corresponding shafts and affustini,
differently designed for each type of weapon (Corino, 1995).

Based on this consideration, a multidisciplinary research
project has been initiated to create a method for systemati
cally studying, protecting, and culturally utilizing military
heritage. Since digital tools are more effective when there is 1
a strong organizational logic behind them (Del Curto &
Grimoldi, 2017), the paper aims to introduce an innovative
protocol that combines the Raumbuch system with the HBIM
methodology.
The Raumbuch method involves breaking down and cat
egorizing an architectural complex into increasingly simpler
parts recognizable by their type and construction history.
These include individual buildings, building blocks, rooms,
and various constructive elements. The collected data are

The goal was to create a highly detailed point cloud (Fig. 2), 1
which was the foundation for creating the Raumbuch and the
subsequent HBIM model.
In the case of La Vallée Palace, the point cloud revealed
some gaps, especially in certain areas along the complex’s
longitudinal walls. To overcome this limitation, it was neces
sary to integrate the data from the point cloud with data from
other sources, such as satellite images, earth photographs,
and drawings from historical archives. This hybrid approach
allowed us to obtain a 3D model of the building that provided
a comprehensive and accurate view of its structure in all its
parts.
In parallel, the Raumbuch method was used to organize
the data collected during the survey (Del Curto, 2007;
Fiorino, 2021). This method involved identifying the build Source: By author
ing, the levels on which the rooms are located, and the rooms
themselves. Each room was then divided into its six main

son cards are divided into six main areas: identification, con Precise qualification vocabularies have been adopted for

This method has made it possible to identify and docu
ment even seemingly insignificant or conflicting elements
and signs. These could easily be overlooked but are, in
fact, valuable evidence of the history and use of the prop
erty. The goal is to accurately define the size of the assets
and assess the effectiveness of the planned measures,
whether they involve maintenance, upgrades, or other
actions. The result is a tool that enables the organized
storage of information within a framework of synthesized
knowledge, which enhances the overall understanding of
the asset.

3 The Case Study of the Former
Garofalo Pasta Factory: Historical 2
Reconstruction and Material Analysis

The Garofalo pasta factory was the first major manufacturing
plant in Gragnano, with a strong connection to the economic
and social history of the area. The former Garofalo pasta fac
tory, due to its considerable dimensions and the high chim
ney tower, constitutes the landmark of Gragnano. Specifically,
it is established as the icon of the fervent industrial reality of
the 1960s.
The investigation was anchored in two pillars: the meticu
lous analysis of hitherto unpublished documents from the
Historical Archive Intesa Sanpaolo and a comprehensive sur
vey of the structure. It was important to understand the spa

Despite its status as a repository of the collective memory
and historical industriousness of Gragnano, the site currently
represents a “negative emergency” for the territory due to its
conditions of degradation and neglected state. The pasta fac
tory is currently in a precarious static condition and in an 10
advanced state of disrepair, caused by the total abandonment
of the area and the lack of adequate roofing systems and fix
tures. This has led to an increase in the presence of rubble
and weedy vegetation.

London (Davies & Joy, 2004; Dyson & Kirk, 2006; Limelite
Heritage, 2021; Pearson, 2019).
2021

4.2 Modernisation of the St. Pancras
Station & the Midland Grand Hotel 6

Integrating modern design and heritage conservation has 2010
been complex during the modernisation of the St. Pancras
Station and Midland Grand Hotel because it required a deli
cate balance between conservation and modernisation. The
underlying theoretical framework of the overall process is
based on architectural adaptation, conservation ethics, and

the present contribution is to investigate the relationship
between a specific asylum typology and the decommis
sioning process, seeking prolonged conditions throughout 1
the “interim time” that elapsed between the ceased origi
nal function and the current state. This issue is inextrica
bly linked to a process triggered after the enactment of the
“Basaglia Law” in 1978, following which a complex and
heterogeneous dismantling of the entire Italian asylum
network was implemented. Assuming these conditions,
the study focuses on the former Rizzeddu Psychiatric 2014
Hospital in Sassari, then expands to other national and
international asylums to identify common features that
could impact potential reuse processes. The investigation 1977
is pursued through various analyses and extended to the
entire complex, with a specific contribution concerning

the assets of decommissioned psychiatric hospitals were to
be used by local health units for income generation through
the sale with the right of first option for public entities.6 It is 4 The Case Study of the Former
believed that the reason for this renegation is to be found in Rizzeddu Psychiatric Hospital
the inadequacy of Italian asylums, which dragged on from in Sassari
the post-World War II onwards, until it became a wellestablished issue even before the enactment of the Basaglia
Law. As early as the 1960s, the asylum represented a true
institutional failure, which turned into a cause for scandal
and institutional shame (Scavuzzo, 2020). The needs
imposed by the new approaches to psychiatric treatments
highlighted the inadequacy of the design tools employed up
to that point. The Basaglia Law then stated the definitive
interruption of the already corrupt dialogue between archi
tecture and psychiatry. Its actuation should have been the
occasion for a renovation process, but it turned instead into a
systematic dismantling of a typology that has been banned
and destined to be forgotten once and for all, as the asylums 7
were liquidated even by public opinion to the point of being
considered a place of oppression of human rights (Scavuzzo, 8
2020). In light of this, Basaglia’s intuition which came at the
dawn of the social and cultural revolution that resulted in
Law 180, is still relevant, as he imagined a reversal of the 1918
institutional mechanism starting from those spatial systems
on which the asylum typologies were based, building a con
ception of order and spatial rigour as a tool for treatment. Its
premises moved precisely from the understanding of a true
institutionalization syndrome, the cause of which is to be
found in the totalizing character that loomed over the patients
(Scavuzzo, 2020). As we know today, a systematic reorgani
zation based on the intrinsic and foundational features of the
asylum institution has never been carried out systematically
and extensively. Law No. 180 was hindered by cross-cutting

The Preservation of Typological Features of Asylum Architecture: From Decommissioning to Reuse 221

and potentialities inherent in former asylums, which are
often overlooked and not consulted properly. The decommis
sioning has interrupted critical and social reflections on the
historical and human memory of the asylum affair, causing a
real disavowal of the asylum’s identity, nature, and historical
reasons. This event has disregarded its own premises, leaving
the heritage to be eroded by time and a collective condemna
tion that still influences the widespread perception of the
asylum’s architectures and images today. The option to reject
certain choices emerges in the sources analysing the state of
asylums in the aftermath of their definitive decommission
ing. This failure to take a stance has led to repercussions on
the architectural qualities, devaluing them, and bending them
to a building process that, when implemented, has often
proved incapable of establishing any dialogue with the heri
tage in question. The case study, located in the former
Rizzeddu mental hospital in Sassari, addresses all the raised
issues, and its design evolved in subsequent construction
phases from an asylum with spaced pavilions to one with
scattered pavilions. This former asylum was a suitable model
to analyse how architectural features were misinterpreted,
due to the incongruous modification of its marginal spaces
by facilities built close to its perimeter, thickening an already
existing distance between the asylum and the city, as well as
further impeding a possible dialogue, therefore bringing out
a clear manifestation of this historical process identified. On

E. Morezzi (*)
between material fabric and commemorative functions creArchitecture and Design Department DAD, Politecnico di Torino,
Torino, Italy ates a complex landscape of meaning. Furthermore, contem
e-mail: emanuele.morezzi@polito.it porary conservation theory by Salvador Muñoz Viñas,

<!-- p. 235 -->

232 E. Morezzi

(Muñoz Viñas, 2005) which highlights the need for negotia 2 The Monument to the Revolution
tion among differing stakeholder values, provides valuable in Leskovac
tools to address the conservation needs of these monumental
sites effectively (Wollentz, 2019). The conservation chal
lenges presented by the monument located in Leskovac, as
an example, not only exemplify the intricate and complex
interplay between material degradation processes and the
various preservation methodologies that can be employed
but also underscore the necessity for a deeply nuanced and
thorough understanding of these multifaceted interactions
(Bassanelli, 2015). The group of memorials designed by 2022
Bogdan Bogdanović to commemorate the victims of World
War II in Serbia forms a distinct and significant series.
Commonly referred to as “spomenik,” these memorials go
beyond mere commemoration, functioning as artistic state
ments that encourage contemplation on memory, sacrifice,
and human resilience. Several key themes recur throughout
Bogdanović’s designs, including integration with the natural
environment. For example, the Slobodište Memorial
Complex in Kruševac utilizes burial mounds and the hilly
landscape to create evocative spaces, while the “Monument
to the Revolution” in Leskovac is situated in a forested park
on a hillside. Another common theme is the use of abstract
symbolism and historical and mythological references. For
instance, the solar arch at Kruševac is viewed both as a portal
to the realm of the dead and an inverted omega, symbolizing
ending and renewal. The winged sculptures in the “Valley of 1
the Living Ones” at Kruševac represent the liberation of
souls, with engravings suggesting the indestructibility of
life. Similarly, the central sculpture at Leskovac, nicknamed
the “Goddess of Victory,” resembles both a female figure and
a Neolithic amphora, while the surrounding stone pylons
recall ancient Balkan tombstones called “stećak.” At the
Mausoleum of Struggle and Victory in Čačak, the triple
megaron serves as a temple, whose gates frame views of the
city and are aligned with the winter and summer solstices,
thus connecting the monument to cosmic cycles of death and
rebirth. The carved beast heads adorning the megaron are
reminiscent of ancient temple decoration practices found in
places like Mesoamerican temples, suggesting a universal
architectural language. Importantly, Bogdanović’s spome
niks eschew ideological iconography, choosing instead to
focus on the shared human experience in the face of tragedy.
This series of memorials stands apart for its unique blend of
abstraction, landscape integration, and historical and mytho
logical symbolism, offering powerful meditations on mem
ory, loss, and the resilience of the human spirit. This essay
will primarily focus on the site of Leskovac due to its distinc
tive architectural and landscape features as well as for the
opportunity it offers to be considered as a case study for a
potential conservation and restoration intervention of this
specific heritage (Stierli, Kulic, 2018). 2017

<!-- p. 238 -->

“To Bring You the Monument Itself.” Bogdan Bogdanović’s Memorials in Serbia and the Tension Between Cultural Value and Denial 235

Recent restoration efforts, beginning in 2015, have addressed
issues of maintenance and conservation, though challenges
remain. The municipality has invested in improved landscap
ing and the removal of graffiti, while plans exist for the res
toration of the site’s lighting systems and the recreation of
decorative gates lost during the 1990s. Regular commemora
tive events still animate the space, particularly on May 9th,
World War II Victory Day, when local government and vet
eran groups gather to honor the past. The site has also adapted
to serve new purposes, as exemplified by its use for the World
Day Against Child Labor celebrations, demonstrating its
ongoing relevance to contemporary social causes.

4.2 Rijeka: Port of Diversity

The reconstruction of Rijeka and its industry in the first postwar decade was very intensive, considering that 70% of the 6
industry was destroyed. During that time, the city developed
into a respectable port and shipbuilding centre of Yugoslavia
(Klen, 1988). Today, tangible and intangible elements of
shipbuilding represent a significant part of the corpus of 2015
Rijeka’s industrial heritage. However, a special place is
occupied by the ship Galeb, which was classified as a cul
tural heritage of the Republic of Croatia in 2006 by the
Croatian Ministry of Culture.
The values of the Galeb ship lie in its architecture and the
socio-historical significance it has acquired by changing 2017
functions from its inception until today. A valuable example
of shipbuilding in its dynamic journey through the seas was,
among other things, the residential yacht of the Yugoslav 7
President and Marshal Josip Broz Tito. The project of a floating museum will keep the ship
anchored in the port of Rijeka and strive to turn it into a cul
tural hub. The City Museum of Rijeka oversees the develop
ment of the concept and scenario of the permanent installation
(Rijeka City Museum, 2020). The principal guideline is to
preserve the authenticity and present it as a kind of pivot of

past, even though it generated its post-war economic devel
opment as a leader in its industry throughout the region. The
transformation of the abandoned factory began without a

However, interdisciplinary critical research has opened new
perspectives. Dealing with the former factory interpretively,
i.e. presenting the industrial socialist culture from the posi
tion of those who worked there, we created an interactive
memory map. By including the audience in its reading, in
terms of value and meaning, a model of interpretation and
presentation of inherited values, primarily intangible, was
developed in transforming the space into a Creative District.
Today, the area lives precisely thanks to the youth commu

sector, including at least a 60% reduction in GHG emissions
by 2030 compared to 2015 levels, and a 16% decrease in the
average energy performance of national residential building
stock by 2030 compared to 2020 levels (EC, 2024).
Several EU initiatives, such as the Liège Declaration (EU,
2024), the EU Urban Agenda (EC, 2019), and the European
Green Deal (EC, 2020), underscore the crucial links between
these challenges, promoting integrated urban development
and prioritising the circular economy, climate adaptation,
and decarbonisation within urban environments.
The interconnected challenges noted above are very evi
dent in Ireland, where there is a persistent housing crisis
(IBEC, 2023), population growth (CSO, 2023b), significant
levels of vacancy (Stokes et al., 2023), and legally binding
carbon reduction targets that are not being met (O’Hegarty &
Kinnane, 2023). The housing crisis has myriad social impacts
for all sectors, for example with nearly 13,900 people access
ing emergency accommodation in 2023 (Peter McVerry
Trust, 2023), an estimated 500,000 adults living with their
parents (Hearne, 2022), and ever reducing affordability. The
crisis is recognised as an impediment to attracting and retain
ing staff, with a detrimental impact on international reputa
tion and the ability to attract inward investment (IBEC,
2023). To address housing demand, the Government’s
Housing for All: A New Housing Plan for Ireland sets a tar
get of constructing 33,000 new homes annually until 2030
(DHLGH, 2021).

2022). If all proposed infrastructure in the National Planning
Framework (Government of Ireland, 2018), including housing,
was built without addressing current embodied carbon inten
sity, the emissions would cancel out any reductions achieved

Towards the Adaptive Reuse of Town Centre Buildings at Scale in Ireland: Introducing the Trebuchet Project 251

3.1 The Role of Vacant, Existing,
and Historic Buildings in Decarbonising
the Built Environment

Decarbonising the existing building stock is essential to meet
the European Union’s emissions reduction targets for the
built environment (EC, 2021). In the EU, 85%–95% of the
existing buildings are expected to still stand by 2050, and
approximately 85% of the current building stock was con
structed before the millenium (EC, 2020). A significant por
tion of this stock includes historic buildings—those built
before 1970—which account for 51% of the EU’s residential
building stock (Gevorgian et al., 2021). Renovating these
existing structures, including historic ones, has significant
potential to advance decarbonisation efforts (Loli & Bertolin,
2021).
Existing buildings, including the vacant ones, store sig
nificant embodied energy and carbon (Armstrong et al.,

over new construction in the context of the housing and cli (Slabik et al., 2023).

To advance circularity principles in cities, policies should aim to redistribute unused and underused building
stock to housing cooperatives and prevent accumulation of building stock (e.g. through introduction of taxes on
speculation and vacancy, limits on per-person housing ownership, and minimum occupancy rates) and foster
non-profit cooperatives and housing systems (e.g. through public procurement practices) (Calisto Friant et al.,
2023).

3.4.1 Conducting LCAs at Scale The literature identifies challenges in conducting LCAs,
including data quality and availability, increased complexity,
and the lack of methodological consensus (Mastrucci et al.,
2017; Lotteau et al., 2015). Collecting detailed information
2015 2017 on construction materials, energy consumption, and trans
portation is particularly difficult, as well as obtaining com
prehensive and comparable data for large-scale assessments
4 (Lotteau et al., 2015).

<!-- p. 257 -->

Towards the Adaptive Reuse of Town Centre Buildings at Scale in Ireland: Introducing the Trebuchet Project 255

Table 3  Governance and policy measures for retrofit at scale

Category Measures (unless otherwise noted, measures are sourced from Wade & Visscher, 2021)
Governance support Encourage consistent and long-term policy; enable central and local government to set targets,
analyse data and monitor building performance; provide support to public sector and non-profit
organisations to deliver large-scale retrofitting.
Data-driven planning Invest in data collection and building quality monitoring tools to support the generation of accurate
data sets; facilitate area-based retrofit planning; use building energy models to plan large-scale
energy retrofitting.
Financial innovation Provision of diverse business models and financial solutions to lower retrofit costs and mitigate
client and occupant risks.
Vocational training Ensure that training programmes align with the skills needed for retrofitting.
Public engagement campaigns Develop national campaigns to inform homeowners, with an emphasis on consumer protection and
risk reduction.
Standardisation versus customisation While standardisation is often advocated to scale up retrofit efforts, customisation of retrofitting
strategies to local context is also important. National schemes may overlook the diversity of local
conditions and limit flexibility, potentially reducing the responsiveness of various groups.
Top-down versus bottom-up approaches While top-down approaches can drive central governance coordination, there is a parallel need for
bottom-up approaches involving local actors and participatory methods to address localised needs
(Hofman et al., 2021). Neither approach alone is sufficient to deliver the systemic change required
for large-scale retrofitting (Hofman et al., 2021).

region’s cities’ infrastructure, by provoking the construction

struction of different architectural building typologies
designed for the well-being of factory workers and their 1939
families, such as social housing buildings, nursery schools,
social services, libraries, company canteens among others.
A remarkable case in the Piedmont is that of Ivrea, today
recognised as a UNESCO World Heritage industrial city,
where, thanks to the ideology developed by the Olivetti com
pany and its influence, various building typologies of high
architectural quality were built to support the development
and well-being of the company workers and their families.
Within these buildings, it is possible to highlight the case of
the Olivetti nursery school built between 1939 and 1941,
located in the area adjacent to the factory and the workers’
housing building. The nursery was designed within the 2
parameters of the rational and modern language, with a clear 1
autarchic character, displayed through the use of the building
materials and furniture. The Olivetti nursery school is a tes 2

In this context, a vulnerability of modern architecture,
avoidable through material documentation, is highlighted.
The lack of shared and accessible manuals or guidelines on
modern materials and the missing information on replicable
methods of intervention for the conservation of modern
architecture needs to be confronted, as a means to avoid the
repetition of partial or complete loss of cases of excellence,
that due to the multiple interventions that have altered their
image (Pretelli, 2021, p. 15), cannot be longer recognised. 5
In the case of the nursery schools F.R.I.G.T. and Olivetti
presented in this paper, as fate would have it, through the
years, these buildings have not been strangers to intervention
procedures. In the case of the Olivetti nursery school, some
of the interventions carried out on the building have contrib

language and some of the original construction materials 5

The University of São Paulo (USP) was founded in 1934,
and along with it, the idea of creating a university city was
conceived. Initially, the University brought together several
existing schools and educational institutions, such as the
School of Medicine and the Law School. Another wellestablished institution was the Polytechnic School, founded
in 1893, where courses in civil engineering, agricultural
engineering, industrial engineering, and architecture were
taught (Santos, 1985). From this program, the Faculty of
Architecture and Urbanism (FAU) of the University of São 1
Paulo was established in 1948, and it was initially located at
Vila Penteado, a mansion designed and built in the early
twentieth century by Carlos Ekman (1866–1940) in the Art 2
Nouveau style, where the graduate program activities of the The materials used in its construction are primarily
