ventive conservation plans, starting with the concept of “cul
tural significance,” which is defined as “the aesthetic,
historic, scientific, or social values for past, present, and
future generations” (Burra Charter, 1979:1). After under
standing the significance, the methodology outlines the
development of safeguard policies and their application to
the property in question.
It is also in the early 1980s that the concept of signifi
cance began to be widely discussed by Anglo-Saxon
researchers, in the search for a methodological approach to
the practical application of the term as a tool for heritage
preservation. An example of this attempt at formulation was
the publication in January 1982 of Conservation Plan: A
Guide to the Preparation of Conservation Plans for Places
of European Cultural Significance, written by the Australian
architectural historian and conservator James Semple Kerr,
which is now in its seventh edition. Kerr, who was part of the
drafting team for the Burra Charter draft discussed at the
ICOMOS-Australia meeting (Kerr, 2013:34), developed in
his work an empirical methodology with an almost manual-
like profile for the preparation of Conservation Management
Plans for cultural properties.
In the wake of these debates, since the last decades of the
twentieth century, the field of preventive architectural conser
vation has gained a growing number of supporters. On the
international stage, one of the pioneering initiatives in the field
philosophy whose goal is to guarantee the survival and lon of preventive architectural conservation was implemented in
gevity of the building. the Netherlands in the 1970s: the Monumentenwacht, a govern
ment-subsidized inspection service for protected historic mon
uments. Also noteworthy is the Maintain our Heritage program

A study conducted in the Milan area on 80 residential buildings—characterized by particular technical or compositional features and designed by
some of the most prominent architects of the second half of the twentieth century—revealed a fairly widespread trend, at least within the Italian
context: the increasing use of non-local materials for facades, along with a frequent reliance on specific surface treatments and processing tech
niques. More than 50 different lithotypes were identified, only a small portion of which had previously been used in traditional Milanese architec
ture (source: Biondelli et al., 2004)

The process of identifying the assets to be protected, which 1
in the Italian context is closely tied to a ‘threshold of histori
cization’, is central to the concept of preservation. A signifi
cant difference emerges between Italy and other European
countries such as England, where the potential buildings to
be listed must meet specific principles. 2
Listed buildings are graded to reflect their relative special
architectural and historic interest (grade I: buildings that are 3
of exceptional special interest; grade II: particularly impor The National Survey identifies the essential characteris
tant buildings of more than special interest; grade III: it tics for recognising relevant buildings based on seven crite
includes buildings of special interest, that require every ria, including the presence of the work in national or
effort to be preserved). The Secretary of State recognises international studies, the role of the building in the evolution
architectural interest in a building of importance in its design, and experimentation of the architectural typology, and the
decoration or craftsmanship. Special interest may also apply design by a prominent figure in the national or international
to particularly significant examples of building types or tech architectural scene. Each building considered by the National
niques and significant plan forms. Artistic distinction and Survey meets at least one of the seven criteria.
engineering/technological interest can be an important con
sideration. Historic interest concerns instead a building that
illustrates important aspects of the nation’s history. On the
other hand, different ‘threshold of historicization’ can
increase protective measures proportionally with the age of 4
the building.
In Italy, the ‘threshold of historicization’ has been estab
lished by the art. 12 para. 1 of the Decree-Legislative No. 42
of January 2, 2004 ( Part II of the Italian Code of Cultural
Heritage and Landscape). This time-limit immediately raises
the issues of the protection of more recent architectural
works, particularly those from the second half of the twenti

The junior high school ‘Valerio Catullo’ is emblematic of
Cecchini’s work reflecting principles and compositional cri
teria that characterise his architectural vision. The building
stands out for its connection with both the urban context and
the tradition. Building in the layered urban environment of
5 6 Verona requires precise choices regarding how to relate to

<!-- p. 297 -->

298 T. Bortolami et al.

historic buildings. The creation of volumes with diverse
forms and heights and curvilinear layouts invites citizens to
walk around the building’s perimeter and to enter only after
experiencing the different views of the historic walls and dis
trict, as well as the nearby medieval basilica. The use of
exposed reinforced concrete is expressed in two variants:
neutral coloured, to distinguish it without overpowering the
layered context, and a deep ochre-coloured, to better harmo
nise with the colours of the historic centre.

Modern Heritage Preservation in the Context of Energy Transition. Twentieth Century School Architecture: A Living Heritage 301

3.2 Enhancement Project

Marco Dezzi Bardeschi defines the restoration work as the
sum of two different aspects: the conservation of the ‘exist
ing’ (as an overall value) and the design of the ‘new’ (as an
added value) (Torsello, 2005). Starting from this reflection,
the research proposes a methodology based on the principle
of ‘minimum intervention’, guiding both the conservation
and the enhancement works. Three main aspects have driven
the design choices:

– The social role of the school, interpreted as a meeting
place and a point of reference for community activities;
– The dynamic didactics (active learning), achieved by
defining more suitable and accessible spaces;
– The focal point of music and theatre, emphasising the
teaching of these arts as an added value. This aim is
expressed through the improvement and revitalisation of
the pre-existing theatres and the addition of new spaces
dedicated to music education.

A crucial issue affecting both the use and the alteration
of school architecture is the challenge of the energy transi
tion. The centrality of this matter has been repeatedly
emphasised, highlighting the need to raise awareness
among architects about the impact of regulations governing
technological transformations in buildings, especially con

cerning energy efficiency goals. In Italy, the automatic,
unplanned application of some of these techniques is lead
ing to the complete disruption of many buildings, espe
cially in northern regions.
Energy efficiency and saving in existing school buildings
represent crucial topics both to reduce operating costs and to
contribute to environmental sustainability goals. In the
European context, this subject is closely linked to the EU
regulations promoting energy efficiency through binding
directives and regulations for member states. The main regu
latory reference is Directive 2012/27/EU on energy effi
ciency (amended by Directive 2018/2002/EU), which sets a
11 common framework of measures to ensure the achievement
of the 32.5% energy efficiency target by 2030. Specifically,
Directive 2010/31/EU of the European Parliament and the
Council on the energy performance of buildings requires that
all public buildings, including schools, achieve high levels of
energy efficiency, gradually moving towards the goal of
nearly zero-energy buildings (nZEB). The most recent
Directive (EU) 2018/844 (amending Directive 2010/31/EU
and Directive 2012/27/EU) introduces additional measures
to improve energy efficiency in buildings and promotes the
integration of digital and smart monitoring technologies in
new buildings and renovations. Unlisted school buildings, as
